# The Violet Court Fragments

> _A text driven tabletop rogue like adventure set in the twilight of a celestial empire.
> The Sun Emperor has not spoken in three hundred years.
> The Court still rules in His name.
> And something hungry is eating its way inward from the borders._

---

## Director's Foreword

Once, the world was whole. The Sun Emperor walked among His people, and the land knew harmony. Then He withdrew into the Spire of Eternal Vigil, and the Celestial Court took up the burden of governance in His absence. That was three centuries ago.

Today, the empire still stands. The Court still issues decrees. The seasons still turn. But something is wrong. The borders are fraying. A corruption called the Crimson Dusk creeps inward, province by province, turning fields to salt and people into hollow things that weep red. The Court calls it a "localized disharmony" and dispatches Harmonization Audits. The provinces that fall are erased from maps. They were never there.

You are a brush scribe of the Eighth Rank, assigned to the Bureau of Harmonious Records. You copy documents. You file reports. You are nobody. But the empire is a machine that grinds nobodies into dust, and the machine is breaking down.

This game is not about saving the world. It is about surviving a system so vast and ancient that no single person understands it anymore. You will make alliances. You will owe debts. You will be tested. You will fail. And when you fall, another scribe will take your place, inheriting your knowledge, your connections, and your enemies.

The surface is dark fantasy. The depths are for those who know how to read.

**Core design vows:**

- Every major figure embodies a way of seeing the world, and those ways are in tension
- Knowledge is the scarcest resource. What you know about whom determines everything
- Ritual Harmony is a measurement, not a virtue. Too little and you are cast out. Too much and you are a threat
- The Dusk advances regardless of your choices. You cannot grind it away
- Death is not an ending. It is how the next cycle begins

---

## The Celestial Empire

### The Nine Domains

The empire spans a continent divided into nine provinces, each ruled by a Provincial Governor who reports to the Court in the capital city, Xiaoyuan.

**The Central Province** surrounds the capital. Here sits the Spire of Eternal Vigil, where the Sun Emperor slumbers or watches or waits. The Court's ministries occupy the Inner City. The Outer City teems with merchants, petitioners, spies, and refugees from provinces that no longer officially exist.

When you first approach the Central Province, the road rises through terraced hills covered in mulberry groves. The air smells of wet earth and woodsmoke mixed with the faint metallic tang of Qi-charged stone. Against the pale sky, the Spire of Eternal Vigil rises like a needle threaded with cloud. It is visible from every corner of the province, a constant reminder of the Emperor's presence and His absence. The city walls of Xiaoyuan glow faintly at dusk, their surface inlaid with Qi conductive stone that hums at a frequency just below hearing. You feel it in your back teeth before you hear it with your ears.

_Silver Ford._ A market town at the junction of the Jade River and the Imperial Highway. Its great wooden bridge, painted with scenes from the founding of the empire, groans under the weight of carts and caravans day and night. Every third building is a shrine to some aspect of celestial governance. The river here runs slow and thick with silt, and fishermen say the fish taste of ink.

_Harmony Rest._ A fortified village that serves as the outer checkpoint for the capital. All goods entering Xiaoyuan pass through Harmony Rest for inspection, taxation, and ritual blessing. The inspection sheds smell of dried fish, ink paste, and the sour sweat of nervous merchants. The chief inspector has a gaze that seems to count your ribs through your robes.

_Moon Brook._ A spa town built around hot springs said to be warmed by the Emperor's own Qi. Wealthy officials retire here to die gracefully. The water tastes of copper and old stone. The inns serve a delicate mushroom broth that is rumored to extend life by years, though no one has ever proved it.

The people of the Central Province dress in muted colors: greys, browns, faded blues. They speak in careful, measured tones and avoid public displays of strong emotion. Their cuisine is the empire's most refined, emphasizing subtle flavors and elaborate presentation. Rice porridge with pickled vegetables for breakfast, steamed buns with lotus seed paste for midday, and elaborate multi-course dinners of braised fish, bamboo shoots, and silken tofu for the wealthy. They celebrate the Festival of the Silent Throne with quiet candlelit processions through the streets, each participant carrying a single flame cupped in their hands.

They fear two things above all: the Spire awakening or failing to awaken. They fear the Court's displeasure more than they fear the Dusk, because the Dusk is still distant and the Court is always near.

Three folktales cling to this province. The Well of Whispers beneath Harmony Rest supposedly contains the voice of the last Emperor before the Sun Emperor; on windless nights, those who listen at its mouth hear prophecies in a language that sounds almost like speech. The Pilgrim Who Walked Backward tells of a farmer who, seeking to avoid his Harmonization Audit, walked backward for three years and found himself at the same door he left, three years older and three years more guilty. The Bride of the Spire is a ghost story about a woman whose lover was taken for instruction; she climbed the Spire's outer wall to find him and was never seen again. During thunderstorms, her silhouette appears in certain upper windows.

Notable figures include Inspector Swift Reed, the chief administrator of Harmony Rest, who has worked there for forty years and maintains a private ledger of everything that has entered the capital and everything that left again. And Widow Gold Thread, a merchant of mourning garments whose shop sits on the Avenue of Eternal Peace. She dresses the dead of the Inner Court and knows more about the true causes of death among the nobility than any physician in the empire.

The Dusk has not yet reached the Central Province. When it does, the Spire's own light will resist the change, creating a long twilight of gold and crimson that lasts for days before the corruption takes hold. The province's relationship with the Court is one of total dependence and quiet terror. They exist to serve the capital, and every person in this province knows the capital would consume them without hesitation if necessity demanded it.

**Deeper into the Central Province.** Beyond the towns and the checkpoints, the Central Province holds landscapes that many travelers never see. The Bamboo Groves of Silent Prayer stretch for miles along the Jade River, their stalks so dense that sunlight filters through in stripes of green and gold. Within these groves, hermits and disgraced officials live in huts of woven bamboo, surviving on river fish and wild greens, waiting for the Court to forget them. The monks of the Golden Orthodoxy maintain small shrines throughout the groves, places where travelers can leave offerings and prayers. Some of these shrines contain messages left by the hermits, coded appeals for help that no one has ever answered.

The river trade is the province's circulatory system. Hundreds of barges move up and down the Jade River every day, carrying grain from the south, ore from the east, timber from the north, and manufactured goods from the capital to the provinces. The barge crews are a distinct class, hard living people who spend more time on the water than on land. They have their own dialect, their own customs, and their own code of honor. They are also the empire's most efficient smuggling network, moving contraband beneath legitimate cargo with the quiet complicity of inspectors who have been bribed, blackmailed, or simply exhausted into cooperation.

**The Merchant Caravans.** The roads of the Central Province are never empty. Merchant caravans move in constant streams, carrying goods from the provinces to the capital and from the capital to the provinces. The caravans are organized by merchant houses that have existed for centuries, their operations regulated by the State Planning Commission. Each caravan consists of a dozen or more wagons, pulled by oxen or the massive grey draft horses that are bred in the Eastern Foundries for heavy hauling. The wagons are painted with the colors and symbols of their merchant houses, creating a moving tapestry of the empire's commercial networks.

The caravan masters are a breed apart. They have seen the empire from end to end, have negotiated with every kind of official and outlaw, and have learned to read people with an accuracy that would shame most Court investigators. They carry news and stories as well as goods, and they are far more knowledgeable about the true state of the empire than any official in the capital. The Court keeps a close watch on the caravan masters, and the caravan masters return the favor.

**The Academic Quarter.** On the western edge of the Central Province, far enough from the capital to avoid constant scrutiny but close enough to receive its benefits, lies the Academic Quarter, a collection of colleges and libraries that train the empire's scholars. The architecture is deliberately humble, low buildings of grey stone set around cobblestone courtyards, but the libraries within are among the finest in the empire. The students here study philosophy, history, and Qi Theory under teachers who have been carefully vetted by the Bureau of Harmonious Narrative. The curriculum is orthodox. The conversations that happen after hours, in the courtyards and the tea houses and the private rooms of sympathetic professors, are less so.

The Academic Quarter has produced more reformers and revolutionaries than any other place in the empire. It has also produced more informers. The tension between these two traditions gives the Quarter its distinctive atmosphere: brilliant, paranoid, and electrically alive with ideas that cannot be spoken aloud.

**The Silent Resistance.** Not all resistance in the Central Province takes the form of open rebellion. Much of it is quieter, more patient, and more difficult for the Court to detect. There are scribes who deliberately introduce small errors into official documents, creating paper trails that lead nowhere. There are ritualists who perform the required ceremonies with precisely incorrect gestures, subtly weakening the Qi structures they are supposed to maintain. There are cooks and servants in the Inner City who listen at doors and pass what they hear to Shadow's network. There are even members of the Golden Orthodoxy who have begun to doubt, quietly, in the privacy of their own hearts, whether the Emperor's edicts are genuine. They do not speak of this doubt. They do not act on it. But it is there, spreading like a slow fire through dry timber, waiting for a spark.

---

**The Eastern Foundries** are a landscape of factory temples where artificer monks tend the empire's industrial soul. The province is a network of valleys and plateaus that have been reshaped by centuries of industry. The mountains have been terraced, hollowed, and connected by bridges and tunnels that form a continuous industrial complex stretching for hundreds of miles. It is said that you could walk from one end of the Foundries to the other without ever seeing the sky.

The first sign that you have entered the Eastern Foundries is the smell. Smoke chokes the sky. Workers chant sutras to the Machine Gods. The Iron Calculation school holds sway here.

The first sign that you have entered the Eastern Foundries is the smell. It hits you before you crest the ridge: hot metal, coal smoke, chemical steam, and something that might be burning hair or burning prayer. The sky is the color of bruised plums, even at noon. The mountains have been terraced into stepped platforms, each one supporting a factory temple that never stops working. The sound is a constant low thunder punctuated by the ringing of hammer on anvil, the hiss of steam vents, and the rhythmic chanting of workers reciting the Calculus of Production.

_Ember Forge._ The oldest factory temple, built into the hollow shell of a mountain that was mined empty three centuries ago. Its bellows are powered by a waterfall that runs perpetually through a channel carved by the first artificers. The heat inside is unbearable. Monks work naked to the waist, their skin scarred by years of stray sparks. They believe that suffering is a form of efficiency.

_Gearheart._ A city built around a central gear the size of a temple. The gear turns once per day, powered by the labor of a thousand prisoners walking treadmills. Each rotation marks a complete cycle of production. The houses of Gearheart are stacked on top of each other like nesting boxes, connected by ladders and walkways that sway in the wind. The poorest workers never see sunlight.

_The Counting House._ A fortress of bureaucracy that tracks every ounce of metal, every worker hour, every prayer uttered in the Foundries. It is staffed entirely by accountants who have taken vows of silence and communicate through abacus clicks and written characters. The Counting House has never lost a single unit of inventory. It has lost whole villages worth of human lives to rounding errors.

The people here eat simply. Bowls of thick noodle soup with slivers of pickled radish and whatever meat is cheapest that week. They drink a bitter tea made from acorns, which stains the teeth brown and is said to improve concentration. They dress in sturdy grey cotton uniforms, patched and repatched, with colored sashes indicating one's position in the production hierarchy. Red for monks, black for engineers, white for overseers, no color for workers.

They celebrate the Festival of the Great Gear, when all production stops for exactly one hour while monks perform the Ritual of Recalibration, aligning every machine with the celestial plan. Workers hate this festival because the lost production is made up by extending the shift the following week.

Their fears are specific: the fear of being replaced by a machine, the fear of the Counting House's audit (which can result in one being classified as "inefficient material" and recycled), the fear of the Qi conduits rupturing, which has happened three times in living memory, each time turning a factory floor into a zone of reality where metal flows like water and flesh crystallizes into ore.

The Eastern Foundries have the Jade Cutter's Lament, a legend about a master artisan who carved a gear so perfect that it offended heaven. The gear was destroyed, and the artisan was forced to spend the rest of his life cutting gears that were deliberately flawed, each one a tiny heresy that he buried in the slag heaps. Some say those buried gears still turn, deep beneath the mountains, and when they align, the earth shakes.

The Ghost Trains are another local dread. Workers speak of empty rail convoys that travel the Foundries at night, their cars filled with the shadows of dead workers. To see a Ghost Train is to be marked for conscription into the next shift of the afterlife.

And there is the tale of the Iron Bodhisattva's First Forging, which claims that the war god's armor was made in Ember Forge and that a shard of that original armor remains embedded in the mountain's heart. Workers sometimes touch the mountain and pray for strength.

Notable figures include Foreman Dead Eye, an overseer whose left eye was replaced with a clockwork lens that sees inefficiency as a visible aura, and Abbot Calculating Wind, the head of Ember Forge, who is 120 years old and kept alive by a machine that breathes for him. His secret is that he has begun to hear the machines whispering in his dreams, telling him to build something terrible.

The Crimson Dusk manifests in the Foundries as a slow rust that spreads across metal surfaces, causing iron to bloom with red crystals that release spores when disturbed. Machinery becomes unreliable, then hostile. Workers who breathe too many spores develop a cough that sounds like grinding gears. The Court's relationship with the Foundries is one of deep dependence. The Iron Calculation school provides the empire's infrastructure, and the empire must tolerate their methods, their ruthlessness, and their secrets.

**The Districts of the Foundries.** The Eastern Foundries are not a single industrial zone but a network of specialized districts, each dedicated to a different form of production. The Forge Rings are where metal is smelted and shaped, a ring of factory temples arranged around a central square where the largest bellows draw air from the surrounding mountains. The Glass Quarter produces lenses, vials, and the delicate Qi conduits that carry power across the empire; the heat here is different, cleaner, and the workers are among the most skilled in the province. The Chemical Flats are where acids, elixirs, and industrial poisons are manufactured; the air is yellow and smells of sulfur, and workers rotate out of the Flats every six months to prevent cumulative exposure, a precaution that does not always work.

Each district has its own hierarchy, its own customs, and its own relationship with the Counting House. The Forge Rings are the most prestigious, the Chemical Flats the most dangerous, and the Glass Quarter the most secretive. Glass workers who leave their district are often followed by agents of the Iron Calculation school, because the techniques they carry in their hands are worth more than gold.

**The Resistance Cells.** Despite the Iron Calculation school's control, resistance survives in the Foundries. Workers have formed secret cells that meet in basements and abandoned tunnels, sharing information about safety violations, planning slowdowns, and occasionally sabotaging equipment. The cells are small, isolated, and constantly at risk of infiltration. They communicate through a network of street vendors and tea sellers who pass messages folded into packets of food. The Iron Calculation school knows the cells exist. It tolerates them because suppressing them entirely would require shutting down production to search every factory, and the loss of output would be unacceptable.

The cells have begun to coordinate with the Common Flame, receiving funding and direction from the Iron Bone's network. The relationship is cautious on both sides. The workers do not fully trust the revolutionaries, and the revolutionaries do not fully understand the workers. But both sides know that when the moment comes, the Foundries will be the first place to rise.

**The Worship of the Machine Gods.** The Machine Gods are not the gods of the old world. They are new, born from the devotion of generations of artificer monks. The primary Machine God is the Great Integrator, the principle of connection and efficiency that binds all industrial processes into a harmonious whole. The secondary deities include the Calibrator (god of precision), the Burner (god of transformation through fire), and the Measurer (god of accounting and balance). The monks perform daily rituals of lubrication, calibration, and recitation, maintaining the gods' favor through correct procedure. The orthodox Golden Orthodoxy views this worship with suspicion, calling it idolatry. The Iron Calculation school calls it efficiency.

---

**The Southern Granary** feeds the empire. Endless rice paddies and mulberry groves worked by tenant farmers who owe everything to the old landholding bloodlines. Conservative. Deeply attuned to the old ways. The Crimson Lineage families keep their ancestral estates here.

Travel south from the capital and within three days the landscape changes. The dry air of the Central Province gives way to humidity so thick you can taste it. The road becomes a raised causeway between flooded fields that stretch to every horizon. The rice is waist high and luminous green, swaying in waves that look almost like breathing. The farmers wear conical hats of woven straw and stand ankle deep in water, backs bent, moving with the slow precision of men who have done the same motion ten thousand times. The air smells of wet earth, flowering mulberry, and the smoke of cooking fires. At dusk, the fireflies rise from the paddies in clouds so dense they look like a second sky of stars beneath the first.

_Jade Terrace._ The largest city in the province, built on a series of carved terraces that rise from the floodplain like a staircase for giants. The upper terraces belong to the Crimson Lineage families, their mansions hidden behind walls painted with scenes of ancestral glory. The lower terraces are a maze of markets, warehouses, and tenements where grain merchants bargain in hoarse whispers.

_Serenity Bend._ A river town famous for its mulberry groves and silk production. The silkworms here are fed on the finest mulberry leaves, and the thread they produce is said to be so strong it can stop a blade. Serenity Bend's weavers are the best in the empire, and their patterns are closely guarded secrets passed down through bloodlines.

_Old Nest._ The ancestral seat of one of the Eight Great Families (the family name is never spoken aloud, only referred to as the House of the Ivory Gate). Old Nest is a walled compound within a walled town, a place of gardens, meditation pavilions, and ancestral shrines. The family has ruled this town for six hundred years and has never been visited by a Harmonization Audit. The locals say this is because they are pure. The smarter locals say nothing at all.

The Southern Granary eats rice in every form: steamed, fried, boiled into porridge, fermented into wine, ground into flour for noodles and cakes. Meals are often communal, with the whole village sitting on the ground around large platters. The staple accompaniment is a fermented fish sauce that smells terrible and tastes like the sea. People dress in practical but beautiful clothing: dark blue or brown tunics with embroidered hems, the embroidery indicating village and family. The wealthy wear silk of dazzling colors, but only in the privacy of their estates; public display of wealth is considered dangerous.

They celebrate the Planting Festival, when the first seedlings of the season are blessed by a representative of the Crimson Lineage, and the Harvest Festival, a week long celebration of music, dance, and competitive eating that culminates in the Selection, where the year's most beautiful young woman is sent to the capital as a "gift" to the Court.

They fear the failure of the rains, the rise of the river, and the Quiet Men who come from the capital to measure fields and count heads. They also fear the old gods of the soil, the ones who were displaced when the Sun Emperor established His order, because those gods are still there, sleeping beneath the roots of the mulberry trees, and they are patient.

Three legends define this province. The Lady of the Drowned Field tells of a bride who was sacrificed to the river in a drought year and still rises on foggy mornings to tend her underwater rice paddy. The Grain That Remembered is a story about a single stalk of rice that grew in the pattern of a face, and when it was harvested, the face was that of a farmer who had been dead for fifty years, and the grain tasted of grief. The Locked Granary speaks of a storehouse built by the first generation that has never been opened, its doors sealed with a lock that has no key; the oldest farmers say it contains the first rice planted in the empire, and if it is ever opened, the land will refuse to bear fruit ever again.

Notable figures include Lady of the Silent Harvest, the current matriarch of the House of the Ivory Gate, a woman who has not spoken a word in public for twenty years and communicates entirely through written notes delivered by mute servants. She is planning something, but no one knows what. And Rice Counter Hu, the chief tax assessor of the province, a man who can estimate the yield of a field by walking its perimeter once, and who has never been wrong. His ledgers contain information about which bloodlines are failing, which lands are exhausted, and which debts can never be repaid.

The Crimson Dusk in the Granary takes the form of blight. It starts at the edges of fields: a redness in the water, a curling of leaves, a blackening of grain. Crops that touch the blight produce hollow kernels that taste of ash. Animals born in Dusk Touched pastures emerge with too many eyes or too few legs. Farmers whisper that the blight is the old gods waking up, hungry after centuries of neglect. The province's relationship with the Court is one of sullen loyalty. They feed the empire, and they are proud of it. But they remember that the empire has never once thanked them. They remember that the Court takes what it needs and calls it taxation.

**Village Life.** A typical village in the Southern Granary consists of thirty to fifty families living in houses of packed earth and thatch, arranged around a central well and a small shrine. The shrine is officially dedicated to the Sun Emperor, but the offerings left there are to older spirits: the River Mother, the Grain Father, the spirits of the ancestors who first cleared this land. The village headman is usually the oldest male, responsible for mediating disputes, organizing communal labor, and dealing with imperial officials. The headman is also the one who must decide how much grain to report to the tax collectors and how much to hide for the village's survival. This decision, made every harvest season, determines whether the village lives or starves.

The daily rhythm is governed by the sun and the season. Planting season means waking before dawn and working until dusk, bent over in the muddy water, placing each seedling by hand. Growing season means constant maintenance: weeding, irrigating, praying. Harvest season is the most intense, a period of back breaking labor that lasts for weeks, followed by the relief of a full granary and the anxiety of the tax collector's visit. The off season is for repairs, marriages, and the slow work of staying alive through the winter.

The divisions between villages are deep and old. Villages hold grudges over water rights, boundary disputes, and marriages gone wrong. These grudges can last for generations, flaring into violence during times of scarcity. The Crimson Lineage families exploit these divisions, playing villages against each other to maintain their control. A village that is fighting its neighbor cannot unite against its landlord.

**The Bloodline Politics.** The Crimson Lineage families of the Southern Granary are not a unified bloc. They are a collection of ancient houses, each with its own territory, its own resources, and its own agenda. The House of the Ivory Gate is the most powerful, but it is challenged by the House of the Crimson Lotus, which controls the eastern river valleys, and the House of the Obsidian Pillar, which holds the southern coastal strip. These houses compete for influence, marriage alliances, and control of the grain trade. Their conflicts are conducted through proxies: manipulated lawsuits, economic pressure, and the occasional assassination framed as an accident.

The younger generation of the Crimson Lineage is a source of constant anxiety for the elders. Many of them have been educated in the capital, exposed to the ideas of the Verdant Path and the Common Flame. Some have begun to question the blood cultivation arts, wondering whether the price of extended life is worth the moral compromise. A few have secretly converted to heterodox beliefs, maintaining the appearance of orthodoxy while privately rejecting everything their families stand for. The elders know this. They do not know what to do about it.

---

**The Western Bulwark** is a chain of fortress monasteries guarding the empire's mountain frontier. The Imperial Garrison trains here. The military's heartland. Soldiers pray to the Iron Bodhisattva before battle.

The air changes as you climb into the Western Bulwark. It becomes thin, cold, carrying the scent of snow and stone and the distant tang of Qi sharpened to a blade's edge. The mountains rise in staggered walls of grey granite, and built into every defensible position is a fortress monastery, its walls reinforced with Qi inscribed bricks that glow faintly at night. The sound of martial chanting echoes through the passes, carried on winds that never stop blowing. Banners of red and gold snap in the gale. This is a landscape built for war, shaped by centuries of conflict, and every stone remembers the taste of blood.

_Sky Pillar Garrison._ The largest fortress in the province, built atop a mountain that was sheared flat by Qi magic in the first century of the empire. It houses ten thousand soldiers, their families, their trainers, and their war machines. The garrison is a self contained city with its own forges, farms (terraced and painstaking), and temples. The soldiers here train from age seven. They know nothing else.

_The Silent Monastery._ A fortress of contemplative warriors who have taken vows of limited speech, communicating through gesture and written characters. They are the empire's finest tacticians. Their library contains every battle manual ever written in the empire, plus commentaries on each. Rumor says they also contain a copy of every battle that never happened, every plan that was too terrible to execute.

_Iron Gate._ The last fortress before the mountain passes that lead into the lawless lands beyond the empire's claim. Iron Gate has never fallen. Its walls are sixty feet thick at the base. Its commander is a woman named Hundred Battles, who has held the post for thirty years and has killed seven assassins sent by the Court to test her loyalty.

The people of the Western Bulwark eat simply and heavily: flatbreads cooked on stones, roasted meat, hard cheese, and a fermented milk drink that tastes like fear and keeps for months. They dress in leather and wool, practical and durable, with metal fittings that serve as emergency armor. Armor is not ceremonial here; it is everyday wear. The wealthy among them are the officers, who wear rank badges of carved jade on their collars.

They celebrate the Feast of the Unbroken Wall, commemorating the defeat of a barbarian invasion four centuries ago, where every soldier who survived was granted a parcel of land. They also observe the Night of the Watch, when all lights in the province are extinguished for one hour at midnight to honor soldiers who died in darkness. Children born on this night are considered blessed by the Iron Bodhisattva.

They fear the Quiet One, a Dusk creature that stalks the high passes and can imitate the voice of anyone it has killed. They fear the Court's attempts to politicize the military, dividing loyalties that should remain pure. They fear the day the mountains themselves stop defending them.

Their legends are martial. The Iron Bodhisattva's Vigil tells of the war god standing watch on the highest peak, spear in hand, waiting for an enemy so terrible that the god has not blinked in six hundred years. The Ghost Legion is a tale of a battalion that was wiped out in a mountain pass but continues to march, seen by lone travelers on misty nights; to join their ranks is to die well. The Sword That Wept speaks of a blade forged from a fallen star that cried blood when its owner was murdered, and that still hangs in the Silent Monastery's armory, occasionally sobbing in the dark.

Notable figures include Marshal Hundred Battles of Iron Gate, who has refused promotion to the capital three times and who corresponds with Iron Wall through encrypted letters that even the Court cannot read. And Brother Wind Counting, a tactician of the Silent Monastery who can predict the outcome of any battle involving known forces with 93 percent accuracy. He cannot predict what happens in the remaining 7 percent, which keeps him up at night.

The Crimson Dusk in the mountains appears as a red frost that kills everything it touches, covering the stone in a layer of crystalline crimson that reflects the sky. The passes become treacherous, the frost making handholds slick and unstable. Patrols that go into Dusk Touched areas sometimes return speaking a language no one has ever heard. The province's relationship with the Court is watchful and conditional. The military serves the Sun Emperor, not the Court. If the Court demands something the military considers dishonorable, they will refuse. This has not happened yet. It is getting closer.

**Training and Discipline.** A soldier of the Western Bulwark begins training at age seven. The first five years are physical: running, climbing, swimming, and the endless repetition of combat forms. The next five years are tactical: reading battle maps, understanding supply chains, learning the principles of formation and maneuver. The final five years are philosophical: studying the Code of the Iron Bodhisattva, a text that defines the ethical obligations of a soldier. The Code requires absolute loyalty to the empire, but it also requires that loyalty be earned; if the empire acts dishonorably, the soldier's obligation shifts from obedience to correction. This provision is not widely discussed in the capital.

The bond between soldiers in the Bulwark is unlike anything found in the rest of the empire. They train together, eat together, and die together. They develop a form of communication that is almost telepathic, based on years of shared experience. In battle, they move as a single organism, anticipating each other's actions, covering each other's weaknesses. This bond is called the Iron Thread, and it is the Bulwark's greatest weapon. The Court fears the Iron Thread because it represents a loyalty that transcends the Court's authority.

**The Beyond the Mountains.** The mountain passes that Iron Gate protects lead to lands that the empire does not control. The official maps show these lands as blank space, labeled "Unclaimed Territory" in neat calligraphy. The soldiers who have been through the passes tell a different story. They speak of a desert of black sand where the sky is permanently overcast, of a city built from the bones of extinct creatures, of a people who worship a god that is also a disease. The Garrison Command maintains a small intelligence unit that monitors activity in these lands, watching for threats that might emerge from the blank spaces on the maps. The unit's reports are classified at the highest level. The few officials who have read them describe them as "troubling."

**The Veterans' Quarter.** In every fortress of the Western Bulwark, there is a section reserved for veterans who have served their time and chosen to remain. These men and women have given their lives to the military and have no place in civilian society. They live in dormitories, receive a small pension, and serve as advisors and trainers to the younger generation. They are the keepers of the Bulwark's memory, the ones who remember battles that have been struck from the records and comrades whose names have been forgotten.

The veterans are a source of unofficial knowledge. They know which officers are corrupt, which strategies have failed in the past, and which threats are real and which are manufactured by the Court to justify military spending. They are also fiercely loyal to the Iron Wall, who they remember as a young soldier who rose through the ranks on merit. If the Court ever moves against him, the veterans will stand with him. The official maps show these lands as blank space, labeled "Unclaimed Territory" in neat calligraphy. The soldiers who have been through the passes tell a different story. They speak of a desert of black sand where the sky is permanently overcast, of a city built from the bones of extinct creatures, of a people who worship a god that is also a disease. The Garrison Command maintains a small intelligence unit that monitors activity in these lands, watching for threats that might emerge from the blank spaces on the maps. The unit's reports are classified at the highest level. The few officials who have read them describe them as "troubling."

---

**The Northern Expanse** is ancient forest and wild Qi. Autonomous peoples live here, some not fully human. The Verdant Path movement began in these woods. The Court tolerates them because the North provides rare reagents found nowhere else.

To enter the Northern Expanse is to leave the empire behind. The cultivated lands give way to forest so old and dense that the canopy blocks the sun, casting everything in a green twilight that never fully brightens. The air is cool and damp, thick with the smell of moss and rotting leaves and something sweet that might be a flower or might be the breath of the forest itself. The Qi here is not the tamed, channeled Qi of the southern provinces. It is wild, living, moving of its own accord. You feel it as a pressure behind your eyes, a hum in your blood, a sense that you are being watched by something that has no face.

_Greenroot._ The only settlement large enough to be called a town, built in and around the roots of a tree so massive that its crown is lost among the clouds. The buildings are grown, not built, shaped from living wood sustained by Qi. The paths between them are suspended walkways that sway twenty feet above the forest floor. The people of Greenroot speak the imperial tongue with an accent that sounds like wind through leaves.

_The Glimmering Pools._ A network of bioluminescent ponds deep in the forest, where the Qi concentration is so high that the water glows with a soft blue light. The forest peoples use the Pools for ritual purification and for the treatment of wounds. The water tastes of electricity and mint. The Court sends expeditions to harvest the glowing algae, which is used in high grade Qi restorative elixirs.

_The Hollow._ A place that is not a settlement but a gathering: a natural amphitheater where the forest peoples convene for the Great Councils every seven years. The Hollow is surrounded by stones carved with pictographs that predate the empire by millennia. No imperial official has ever attended a Great Council. The forest peoples consider this a sign of wisdom.

The people of the Northern Expanse do not "eat" in the imperial sense. They take sustenance from the forest through a practice they call Rooting: sitting in meditation beneath a tree, they absorb Qi from the soil and nutrients from the air. They do also eat physical food, mushrooms and wild grains and a purple root vegetable that tastes of honey, but the act is ceremonial rather than necessary. They dress in clothing woven from plant fibers that shift color with their mood, a property the Court's alchemists have never been able to replicate. They wear no shoes. Their feet are hard as horn.

They celebrate the Greening, when the first new growth of spring is honored with a festival of music and storytelling that lasts three days. They also observe the Stillness, a day of complete silence at the height of winter, when the forest is sleeping and the people must not wake it.

They fear the Court more than they fear the Dusk, because the Court they understand (a machine that consumes what it does not understand) and the Dusk they do not yet fear. They are wrong about this, and some of the older forest speakers know they are wrong. They also fear the Quiet Places, pockets of deadened Qi left by ancient catastrophes, where the forest refuses to grow and the air is heavy with the memory of violence.

Their legends are the oldest in the empire. The First Root tells of a tree that grew at the beginning of the world, its roots connecting all living things, and that one root still survives somewhere in the deep north, waiting for someone worthy to find it. The Singing Earth speaks of a time before language, when everything communicated through vibration, and the stones still remember how to sing if you press your ear against them at the right moment. The Walking Mothers are tales of ancient forest beings, something like trees and something like women, who walk the forest when the empire's cruelty becomes too great, and who will one day walk south.

Notable figures include Keeper of the First Root, the eldest of the forest speakers, who is said to be over seven hundred years old and who has been waiting for someone to ask the right question for six centuries. And Moss Gatherer, a young forest woman who serves as the Expanse's emissary to the Court and who finds the bureaucracy so alien that she has begun to dream in imperial forms, a condition the forest speakers consider a kind of sickness.

The Crimson Dusk in the Northern Expanse takes the form of a slow weeping. The trees begin to bleed a crimson sap. The bioluminescent pools turn red, then black. The animals grow confused and lost, returning to settlements with wild eyes, unable to speak even in the limited way that forest animals can speak. The forest speakers have begun to hear something in the red wind, a voice that might be the Dusk itself, and they are trying to decide whether it is singing or screaming. The province's relationship with the Court is one of uneasy trade. The Court needs the Expanse's reagents. The Expanse tolerates the Court's presence. Neither side trusts the other. Both sides are right.

**The Tribes of the Expanse.** The Northern Expanse is not a single nation. It is a collection of tribes and communities, each with its own territory, customs, and relationship with the forest. The Root Speakers are the most numerous, living in settlements like Greenroot and maintaining the closest relationship with the forest's Qi. The Wind Walkers are nomads who follow the migratory patterns of the great forest herds, living in tents of woven bark and moving with the seasons. The Deep Ones are the most mysterious, living in the Expanse's darkest depths, rarely interacting with outsiders; they are said to have developed abilities that allow them to perceive the thoughts of animals and the memories of stones.

Each tribe sends representatives to the Great Council at the Hollow every seven years. The Council discusses matters of common concern: the advance of the Dusk, the demands of the Court, the health of the forest. Decisions are made by consensus, a process that can take weeks. The Court finds this maddeningly inefficient. The forest peoples consider it the only way to make decisions that will hold.

**The Spirit Practices.** The forest peoples do not worship gods in the imperial sense. They interact with spirits, the animating forces of the natural world. Every tree, every river, every stone has a spirit, and these spirits must be acknowledged, respected, and occasionally negotiated with. The forest peoples communicate with spirits through dance, song, and the careful interpretation of natural signs. A leaf falling in a particular pattern, the direction of a bird's flight, the taste of water from a particular stream, all of these carry meaning.

The most powerful spirit practitioners are the Speakers, who can enter trance states that allow them to perceive the spirit world directly. In trance, a Speaker can travel across the Expanse in moments, communicate with spirits from distant territories, and sometimes glimpse the future. The trance state is dangerous. Some Speakers do not return, their bodies found sitting peacefully among the roots, their minds gone to places from which there is no return. The Court has tried to capture Speakers and force them to serve imperial interests. The attempts have failed. The Speakers cannot be forced. They can only be asked, and they can only refuse.

---

**The Border Provinces** were four. Now they are two. The others have fallen to the Crimson Dusk and been struck from official records. The remaining border governors send increasingly desperate reports. The Court files them under "Resolved."

The four border provinces were named Scorch Field, Amber Shore, Weeping Valley, and Gate's End. Two remain. The names are not important, the Court insists. They were never important.

Scorch Field fell first, thirty eight years ago. It was a dry, hot province of grasslands and herders, where the people lived in portable tents of felt and followed the rains. The Dusk appeared as a brushfire that never went out, advancing at the speed of a walking man, leaving behind a plain of black glass that reflected the red sky. The evacuation was partial. The Court declared the province "resettled" and redirected its military budget. The herders who escaped fled east, carrying stories that no one in the capital wanted to hear.

Amber Shore fell twenty years later. It was a coastal province of fishing villages and salt flats, known for its amber colored sunsets and the migratory sea creatures that would beach themselves during certain Qi alignments. The Dusk rose from the sea. The water turned red, then thick, then solid. The fishing fleet returned to port one evening with crews who could not remember their own names. The harbor is now a field of rusted ships embedded in red crystal. The Governor sent seventeen reports before the silence. The seventeenth was a single sentence: "The tide does not go out anymore."

Weeping Valley and Gate's End remain. Weeping Valley is a narrow, fertile valley between two mountain ranges, known for its waterfalls and its vineyards. The wine from Weeping Valley was once served at the Emperor's own table. Now the valley's eastern third has been consumed by a crimson fog that never lifts, and the winemakers say the grapes that touch the fog produce a wine that tastes of copper and screams. The Governor has begun fortifying the valley's narrow points, building walls of stone and petitioning the capital for military support that never arrives.

Gate's End is the empire's last true border, a fortress city built into a mountain pass that leads into lands the empire never conquered. The people here are hardy, suspicious, and independent. They have watched two neighboring provinces fall and have drawn their own conclusions. They have begun hoarding weapons, food, and knowledge. The Governor of Gate's End has not sent a report in six months. The Court has not noticed.

The people of the remaining border provinces eat what they can. Weeping Valley's cuisine is built around its wine, used in cooking, drinking, and ritual blessing. Gate's End eats preserved meat, hard bread, and a savory paste made from fermented beans that keeps for years. They dress in practical, patched clothing, often made from the uniforms of dead soldiers. They have no festivals anymore, only funerals and the somber gatherings called Councils of Continuing, where they decide who will stay and who will flee.

They fear the fog, the red sky, the knock at the door that comes at night. They fear the Quiet Ones, the Dusk Touched people who wander out of the consumed lands wearing the faces of the dead. They fear being forgotten more than they fear dying, because the Court's greatest punishment is not death but erasure, and they have seen it happen to entire provinces.

Their legends are recent and raw. The Last Messenger of Scorch Field is a story about a courier who ran for seventeen days to deliver a warning that no one read; he is still running, somewhere in the Dusk, still carrying his undelivered message. The Red Fisherman of Amber Shore tells of a figure who walks on the solid sea, casting a net made of light, pulling in things that should not exist. The Gate That Waits is from Gate's End itself: a prophecy that when the final border falls, a hidden gate in the mountain will open, and something that was sealed away before the empire was founded will emerge.

Notable figures include Governor of the Last Stand of Weeping Valley (he has abandoned his personal name and answers only to his title), who spends his days walking the walls and speaking with each soldier personally, learning their names, knowing that he will one day write letters to their families. And the Keeper of the Gate at Gate's End, a woman whose true name is unknown even to her closest advisors, who claims to have dreamed of the Dusk before it appeared and who has been preparing for this moment her entire life. She has never been wrong about a prophecy.

The Crimson Dusk in the border provinces is not a future threat. It is a present one. The fog advances by inches, sometimes by feet, sometimes by miles overnight. The people who live here do not ask whether the Dusk will come. They ask whether their children will recognize them when it does. Their relationship with the Court has shifted from loyalty to something harder, colder, and more desperate. They no longer serve the empire. They survive in spite of it.

**Weeping Valley in Detail.** The valley is named for the sound its waterfalls make, a constant, mournful roar that fills the air like a lamentation. The valley runs east to west, a green gash between two ranges of grey stone, fed by the snowmelt that cascades down the surrounding peaks. The vineyards cover the southern slopes, their terraces carved into the hillside by generations of winemakers. The wine cellars are tunneled into the bedrock, where the temperature remains constant and the casks age in darkness.

The people of Weeping Valley are proud, stubborn, and deeply attached to their land. They have lived here for centuries, growing grapes and raising families and paying their taxes. They have watched the eastern third of their valley disappear into red fog, and they have not fled. They have built walls. They have trained their children to fight. They have stockpiled food and medicine. They will defend their home until the last vineyard is consumed.

The Valley's Governor is a man who has lost his name and his family to the Dusk. His wife and children were in the eastern third when the fog advanced. He does not know if they are alive or dead. He has not stopped fighting.

**Gate's End in Detail.** Gate's End is not a beautiful city. It is a functional one, built for defense and survival. The walls are thirty feet thick at the base, tapering to fifteen at the top, made of stone that was quarried from the mountain itself. The city is built in layers, each one a fallback position if the previous layer is breached. The streets are narrow, designed to channel attackers into killing zones. The houses are built of stone, with flat roofs that serve as fighting platforms.

The people of Gate's End are a mix of cultures, drawn from every province. They have been shaped by the constant threat of annihilation into something new: hard, capable, and utterly without illusions about the nature of the empire. They know the Court will not save them. They have accepted this. They will save themselves.

The Keeper of the Gate is the most powerful person in the city, though she holds no official title. She is a prophet, a strategist, and a leader. She has predicted every major Dusk advance within a margin of days. She has prepared the city for each phase of the crisis with a precision that seems almost supernatural. The people trust her absolutely, because she has never been wrong.

**The Daily Reality of the Border.** In Weeping Valley, every morning begins with a check of the fog line. Farmers climb to their rooftops and look east, measuring the distance the crimson mist has advanced overnight. Some days it has not moved. Some days it has swallowed another mile. There is no pattern, no predictable schedule, and the unpredictability is worse than steady advance would be. People have learned to live in a state of permanent alertness, their bags packed, their escape routes planned. Children born here have never known a world where the red was not on the horizon.

Gate's End has become something unexpected: a thriving community shaped by crisis. The constant threat has stripped away everything unnecessary. Social hierarchies have flattened. The old divisions between rich and poor, official and commoner, have been replaced by a single distinction: those who will fight and those who will flee. The Keeper of the Gate has instituted a system of universal training, requiring every able bodied person to learn basic combat and survival skills. The result is a population that is harder, more capable, and more united than any other in the empire.

**The Refugees.** The refugees who have escaped the fallen provinces are scattered across the empire, but the largest concentration is in the camps outside Gate's End. These camps are temporary in name only; some have existed for years. The refugees live in tents of patched canvas, surviving on rations distributed by the Garrison and whatever work they can find. They carry the memories of what they saw, stories that they tell to anyone who will listen and that most listeners wish they had not heard.

The refugees have formed their own communities within the camps, organized around the provinces they came from. The Scorch Field survivors maintain their herding traditions, raising goats and sheep in the thin grasslands near the camps. The Amber Shore survivors have built a fleet of small fishing boats on a lake that is nowhere near the sea, sailing them in memory of the ocean they lost. These communities are insular, suspicious, and fiercely protective of their memories. They are also the only sources of reliable information about what the Dusk is really like, what it does to people, and what happens to the lands it consumes. The Court does not interview them. The Court does not acknowledge their existence.

---

### Celestial Qi

The world is woven from Qi, a vital energy that flows through earth, sky, flesh, and spirit. The Court's founding doctrine holds that only the Court, through correct ritual practice, can channel Qi safely. Deviation from orthodoxy causes Disharmony, a reality warping catastrophe where the laws of nature become unstable.

This is not entirely false. Disharmony events are real and terrifying. What the Court obscures is whether their monopoly prevents Disharmony or merely controls who gets to define it.

#### The Five Aspects of Qi

Qi is not a single substance but a confluence of five distinct aspects, each with its own nature, its own flow patterns, and its own relationship with the world. The five aspects are not separate energies but dimensions of a single underlying force, like colors of light that combine to form white. A master of Qi can perceive all five simultaneously, reading the world as a constantly shifting pattern of colored flows. Most people perceive only the dominant aspect of their environment, and many perceive nothing at all, their sensitivity dulled by the deadening influence of city life.

The understanding of the five aspects is not universal. Different schools emphasize different aspects, and this emphasis shapes their entire worldview. The Golden Orthodoxy focuses on Binding Qi, seeing stability as the highest good. The Verdant Path reveres Yielding Qi, valuing growth and adaptation. The Iron Calculation harnesses Burning Qi for industry and production. The Bright Mirror channels Piercing Qi in her investigations of truth. The Crimson Lineage, uniquely, cultivates all five through the medium of blood, which they believe contains the essence of all aspects in their purest form.

**The Piercing Aspect.** Qi of clarity, truth, and revelation. Those who cultivate this aspect develop an ability to perceive hidden connections, to see through deception, and to recognize patterns that others miss. At the highest levels, a Piercing adept can perceive the true nature of any person or object, seeing beyond appearance to essence. This is not always a gift. The knowledge of what people truly are can be devastating. Many Piercing adepts withdraw from society, unable to bear what they see. The aspect is associated with the element of wind, which clears away obscuring mists, and its color is silver white. It flows strongest at dawn, when the boundary between dream and waking is thinnest. It is associated with the element of light, the direction of east, and the season of spring. Those who cultivate Piercing Qi can see through lies, perceive hidden connections, and, at the highest levels, glimpse the true shape of reality beneath the veil of appearances. The Bright Mirror school relies heavily on this aspect, using it to detect corruption and Disharmony. In daily life, ordinary people experience Piercing Qi in moments of sudden understanding, when a problem resolves itself in the mind with crystalline clarity.

**The Yielding Aspect.** Qi of growth, healing, and transformation. This aspect governs all processes of change and renewal: the growth of crops, the healing of wounds, the turning of seasons. Cultivators of Yielding Qi are sought after as healers and midwives, but the aspect has martial applications as well. A Yielding adept can accelerate the decay of an opponent's armor, encourage infections in wounds, or cause a building's foundations to rot. The aspect is deeply connected to water, which takes the shape of its container while remaining essentially unchanged. Its color is green. It flows strongest in the humidity of summer rain, and it is associated with water, the west, and the season of autumn. This is the aspect that makes crops grow and wounds heal. The Verdant Path school reveres the Yielding Aspect above all others, seeing it as the fundamental life force of the world. Ordinary people experience Yielding Qi in the feeling of health returning after illness, in the first rain after a drought, in the quiet satisfaction of watching something grow.

**The Binding Aspect.** Qi of structure, law, and permanence. This aspect gives form to matter and stability to institutions. Binding Qi is what holds buildings upright, what makes contracts binding, what keeps the empire from dissolving into chaos. Cultivators of Binding Qi become rigid in their thinking, resistant to change, and deeply committed to established order. This is not necessarily a flaw; the empire depends on such people. The aspect is associated with earth and stone, which endure while everything else changes. Its color is brown, shading to amber in its purest form. It flows strongest at midnight, when the world is still and forms are most fixed. It is associated with earth, the center, and the depth of winter. This is the aspect that gives form to matter, that holds buildings upright, that keeps the empire's bureaucracy running. The Golden Orthodoxy considers the Binding Aspect the most important, because it represents stability and the preservation of established order. Ordinary people experience Binding Qi in the feeling of solid ground beneath their feet, in the weight of a well made tool, in the satisfaction of a completed contract.

**The Burning Aspect.** Qi of passion, conflict, and change. This aspect fuels everything that consumes and transforms: fire, industry, combat, desire, ambition. Burning Qi is the most immediately powerful aspect and the most dangerous. It is easy to channel and difficult to control. Cultivators of Burning Qi are prone to outbursts of temper, obsessive behavior, and a condition called Ash Heart, where the passion burns so hot that it consumes the person from within, leaving an empty shell driven by pure habit. The aspect is associated with fire and forge, and its color is red. It flows strongest at noon, when the sun is at its zenith and the world is most alive. It is associated with fire, the south, and high summer. This is the aspect that fuels combat, industry, and desire. The Iron Calculation school harnesses the Burning Aspect in its forges and factories, while the Common Flame channels its heat into revolutionary fervor. Ordinary people experience Burning Qi in the rush of anger, the heat of exertion, the fire of ambition, and the warmth of love.

**The Veiling Aspect.** Qi of mystery, concealment, and potential. This aspect governs everything hidden, unrealized, or in between states. It is the Qi of twilight, of dreams, of the unborn, of secrets. Cultivators of Veiling Qi become adept at concealment, illusion, and perception of hidden truths. They are the empire's spies, its infiltrators, its shadow dancers. The aspect is deeply unsettling to those who specialize in other aspects, because Veiling Qi feels like absence, like a hole in the world where something should be. Its color is violet black, the color of the sky in the moment between sunset and full night. It flows strongest at twilight, in the moments between day and night. It is associated with shadow, the north, and the brief season that the empire does not officially recognize, the hinge time between autumn and winter. This is the aspect of secrets, of things hidden and things not yet born. The Shadow operates entirely within Veiling Qi. Those who cultivate it can move unseen, conceal truths, and perceive the unrealized possibilities in any situation. Ordinary people experience Veiling Qi in the feeling of being watched in an empty room, in the flicker of movement at the edge of vision, in the sense that something important is about to happen.

#### Qi in Daily Life

Every person in the empire, from the Grand Council elder to the street sweeper, interacts with Qi constantly, whether they know it or not. Breathing is a Qi practice, though most people do it imperfectly. The imperial standard for proper breathing, taught to all children in Academy schools, involves inhaling through the nose in a measured rhythm and exhaling through the mouth, visualizing the breath moving through the body's channels. Those who breathe incorrectly, the orthodoxy teaches, accumulate stagnant Qi that leads to illness, confusion, and vulnerability to Disharmony.

Morning routines across the empire involve Qi practices. The wealthy employ Qi therapists who perform resonant massage, moving their hands along the body's channels to correct blockages. The poor perform simple self Qi exercises: stretching, tapping certain points on the body, humming at specific frequencies. Tea is never just tea; it is a Qi infusion. Different teas are prescribed for different Qi imbalances. Green tea for excess Burning, white tea for deficient Yielding, black tea for blocked Binding.

Sleep is understood as a period when the Veiling Aspect predominates and the soul's Qi drifts loose from the body. Nightmares are considered contact with Disharmonious Qi fields. Waking refreshed is a sign of healthy Qi circulation. The Court mandates that all imperial citizens sleep with their heads pointed north, to align with the flow of celestial Qi. Violations are minor offenses, but they are recorded. Three violations result in a warning. Five violations trigger an investigation into whether the violator is deliberately cultivating Disharmony. The investigation is rarely favorable.

The practice of Feng Shui, the arrangement of physical spaces to optimize Qi flow, is an integral part of daily life across all classes. Wealthy families employ professional Feng Shui masters to design their homes and gardens, ensuring that the five aspects circulate properly. Poor families make do with limited adjustments: placing mirrors to deflect negative Qi, hanging wind chimes to disperse stagnant energy, orienting doors and windows according to traditional formulas. The Court officially endorses Feng Shui as a practical science while privately viewing excessive attention to it as a sign of superstitious tendencies that border on heterodoxy.

Qi astrology is another common practice. Every person's birth is associated with a specific Qi configuration based on the time, date, and location of their birth. This configuration is believed to influence personality, fortune, and compatibility with others. Marriage negotiations include careful comparison of Qi charts, and parents consult astrologers before naming their children. The Court tolerates Qi astrology as a folk practice while officially discouraging it, because accurate predictions of future events would undermine the Court's control over the narrative.

#### Orthodox and Heterodox Qi Manipulation

The Court recognizes four approved methods of Qi manipulation.

**Ritual Chanting.** The safest and most common method. Specific sequences of sounds, when pronounced correctly, shape Qi in predictable ways. The chants are closely guarded, taught only to licensed ritualists. Using an unlicensed chant is a serious crime.

**Acupuncture and Moxibustion.** The insertion of needles or application of heat at specific points on the body to redirect Qi flow. Practiced by licensed physicians only. The needles are made of different metals depending on which aspect of Qi is being treated: silver for Piercing, copper for Yielding, iron for Binding, gold for Burning, obsidian for Veiling.

**Inscription.** The writing of Qi conductive characters on surfaces. The characters must be written in a specific order, with specific brushstrokes, using ink infused with the appropriate Qi aspect. Inscription is how Qi conduits, fortress walls, and official documents are empowered.

**Meditation and Alignment.** The direct manipulation of one's own internal Qi through mental discipline. This is the most difficult method and the most respected. Masters of meditation can achieve effects that rival the most complex rituals.

Beyond these approved methods lie the forbidden arts. Blood cultivation, the signature practice of the Crimson Lineage, involves the consumption and manipulation of blood to enhance one's Qi. The Lineage claims its methods are hereditary and cannot be learned by outsiders, a claim that is not entirely false but is also convenient for maintaining their monopoly. Spirit binding involves making agreements with non human entities, usually nature spirits or the ghosts of the dead, to borrow their Qi. The agreements are dangerous because the entities have their own agendas and are patient and deceitful negotiators. Qi draining, the most forbidden practice, involves forcibly extracting Qi from another living being and consuming it. The practice is physically addictive, morally corrosive, and ultimately unsustainable because drained Qi carries traces of the original owner's consciousness. Prolonged drainers begin to hear the voices of everyone they have drained, a condition called the Echo Chorus that eventually drives them mad.

Heterodox methods include blood cultivation (the Crimson Lineage's hereditary practice of enhancing Qi through consumption of blood, ideally from one's own bloodline), spirit binding (forcing agreement with non human entities to borrow their Qi), and the forbidden practice of Qi draining, where one person's Qi is forcibly extracted and consumed by another. Qi draining is punishable by immediate Harmonization. It is also more common than the Court admits.

The deepest forbidden knowledge is the Theory of Fundamental Siphon, which suggests that the entire empire is a vast Qi draining apparatus, that the Sun Emperor's original gift was not a gift but a loan, and that the Crimson Dusk is the debt coming due.

#### Qi Geography

Qi flows through the land in patterns that the Bureau of Celestial Inquiry has mapped for centuries. The maps are considered state secrets, but fragments have leaked.

The Central Province sits on a convergence of all five aspects, which is why the Spire was built there. The convergence creates a zone of unusually stable Qi, ideal for ritual work but vulnerable to stagnation. The Court's rituals constantly stir the Qi to prevent it from pooling.

The Eastern Foundries are rich in Burning and Binding Qi, which makes them ideal for industry but leaves the population prone to Burning aspect imbalances: aggression, burnout, and a condition called forge madness where workers become obsessed with production to the point of self destruction.

The Southern Granary is dominated by Yielding Qi, which makes the land fertile and the people calm, but also resistant to change. The Crimson Lineage families maintain their power partly through their ability to access the deeper veins of Binding Qi that run beneath the rice paddies.

The Western Bulwark channels Piercing and Burning Qi through its peaks, creating natural amplifiers for combat magic but also making the region prone to sudden storms of wild Qi that can strip a person's memories.

The Northern Expanse is the most Qi diverse region, with wild currents of all five aspects flowing in unpredictable patterns. This makes it dangerous for conventional ritual work but invaluable for gathering rare Qi aligned reagents. The forest peoples have learned to ride these currents rather than control them, a skill the Court's ritualists have never mastered.

Qi geography has practical implications for travel and communication. Moving through regions of mismatched Qi can cause fatigue, confusion, and physical illness. The empire's major roads are built along Qi alignments, following natural channels that minimize resistance. Travelers who stray from these roads can find themselves in pockets of stagnant or wild Qi that produce strange effects: accelerated aging, memory loss, spontaneous Qi manifestation. The Court maintains detailed maps of safe routes and Qi hazards, but these maps are classified and only available to authorized travelers.

Qi earthquakes are a poorly understood phenomenon in which the Qi of a region suddenly destabilizes, producing effects similar to a physical earthquake but with added strangeness: buildings collapse, the sky changes color, and people experience shared hallucinations. Qi earthquakes are rare but devastating. The last major one, forty years ago in the Eastern Foundries, killed three thousand workers and left a crater where a factory had stood. The Bureau of Celestial Inquiry has developed early warning systems that can predict Qi earthquakes hours in advance, but the systems are expensive and unreliable, and only the Inner City has them.

#### Qi and Emotion

The relationship between Qi and emotion is bidirectional and poorly understood. Strong emotions generate Qi, especially Burning Qi from anger and Veiling Qi from fear. Conversely, imbalances in Qi can produce emotional states. Excessive Binding Qi leads to rigidity and stubbornness. Deficient Yielding Qi leads to inability to heal from grief.

The Court teaches that emotion should be moderate because extreme emotion produces unstable Qi that attracts Disharmony. The Verdant Path argues that this teaching is convenient for a ruling class that wants its subjects docile. The Common Flame demonstrates, through the example of the Iron Bone and his wolf spirit, that channeled rage can be a source of tremendous power.

The forbidden insight, known only to those who have studied the deepest archives, is that the Crimson Dusk may be nothing more than accumulated human emotion, the suffering of millions of people over centuries, condensed into a substance that has begun to think.

The Five Emotions correspond to the Five Aspects in traditional Qi Theory: joy resonates with Burning Qi, grief with Yielding Qi, fear with Veiling Qi, anger with Piercing Qi, and contemplation with Binding Qi. When an emotion is experienced purely and fully, it strengthens the corresponding aspect in the environment. When an emotion is suppressed or falsified, it creates a disturbance that can attract Disharmony. This is why the Court's emphasis on emotional restraint is so dangerous. The Court teaches that emotion should be moderated because extreme emotion produces unstable Qi, but the suppression itself creates the instability it claims to prevent. The Verdant Path teaches that emotions should be expressed and released, allowing the Qi to flow through them and pass. The Common Flame teaches that emotions, especially rage, should be channeled into action, transforming personal feeling into collective power.

The most controversial theory, advanced by a scholar who was subsequently Disappeared, is that the Crimson Dusk is the accumulated suppressed emotion of the empire, the grief and rage and fear that the Court has forbidden its subjects to express, condensed over centuries into a living substance. According to this theory, the Dusk is not a corruption but a release, the empire's emotional pressure valve finally blowing open. The theory has never been disproven. It has only been suppressed.

#### Qi and the Dusk

The Dusk behaves like Qi in all observable respects, except that it is red, hostile to life, and apparently intelligent. The orthodox theory holds that the Dusk is Disharmony given form, a punishment for heterodox practices in the border provinces. The unorthodox theory, whispered among scholars who value truth over safety, is that the Dusk is what happens when Qi is drained from a place completely, leaving a void that nature abhors and fills with something else.

Celestial Book, who has read every text on the subject, has never offered an opinion. When asked directly, he quoted a text that no one present had ever heard of: "The wine does not turn to vinegar by accident. The cask was built for wine, but it was also built for vinegar, and the cellar master knew this from the beginning."

---

### The Sun Emperor's Silence

Three hundred years ago, the Sun Emperor entered the Spire of Eternal Vigil and sealed the doors behind Him. He has not emerged. His edicts still arrive, inscribed on jade tablets that materialize in the Throne Chamber. The Court executes them without hesitation.

Or so the official narrative maintains.

The truth is one of the central mysteries. Each playthrough may reveal a different facet: Was He betrayed? Did He choose silence? Was there ever an Emperor at all? The answer shapes the endgame.

---

### The Sealing

Do not resolve this. Everything below is what people experienced, and none of it explains what happened, because nobody knows what happened, including the Duke of Eternal Night, who was standing eleven paces away.

What follows is why the empire cannot let go.

#### What It Was Like to Have Him

He answered questions.

That is the thing three hundred years of theology has almost successfully buried, and it is the only fact that matters for understanding the grief. The Sun Emperor was not a remote divinity attended by intermediaries. For two hundred and eighty seven years, on the first and fifteenth of every month, He sat in the Hall of Open Hearing, and anyone at all could come and ask Him a question, and He would answer it.

Anyone. There was no petition, no rank requirement, no filtering. The queue formed in the night. A woman with a dispute over an irrigation ditch stood behind a Grand Council elder and in front of a boy who wanted to know why his brother had died, and all three were heard, in order, at whatever length the answer required.

He was not oracular. He did not speak in riddles. Witnesses across three centuries of record agree with unusual consistency that He was patient, specific, and slightly dry, that He asked clarifying questions, that He would say plainly when He did not know something, and that He had a way of pausing before answering which made the person asking feel that the question had been worth the pause.

He was seen. That is the other thing. He walked. He attended the Lantern Festival every year and stood in the crowd, and the crowd did not part for Him, because He had asked, once, four hundred years earlier, that it not. There are eleven independent accounts of Him buying roasted chestnuts from street vendors, and the vendors' descendants still trade on it, and at least four of those families are lying, and nobody minds.

The empire under a present Emperor was not a utopia. There were famines, wars, and bad governors. What there was not, anywhere in the Nine Domains, was the question _what would He want?_ You could go and find out. Twice a month, in person, in a queue.

Understand what that did to a civilization. There was no interpretation. No school of doctrine, no competing readings, no priesthood of the text, because there was no text: there was a man in a hall on the fifteenth, and if you disagreed with someone about what was right, the argument had an end.

Every institution in this book exists because that ended.

#### The Last Year

There was no final speech. This is the detail that undoes people.

In the eleventh month of Year 287 He heard forty one questions, of which the last was a dispute between two ritual bureaus about the correct color of the thread used to bind the summer offerings: whether it should be the deep red of the old northern practice or the vermilion adopted three centuries earlier. The dispute was ninety years old and genuinely bitter.

He heard both sides. He asked what dye each bureau actually used, and where it was sourced, and what it cost. He then ruled for the vermilion, on the grounds that the northern dye required a beetle that had become scarce, and that a rite which depends on a scarce thing eventually becomes a rite about scarcity.

That is the last recorded sentence of the Sun Emperor. It is about beetles. It is careful, sensible, faintly funny, and completely ordinary, and there are people in the capital who have wept reading it, because He clearly did not know either.

#### The Announcement

In the second month of Year 288 He informed the Grand Council that He would enter the Spire of Eternal Vigil for a period of meditation, and that the doors should be closed behind Him.

Nobody was alarmed. He had done this before. There are nine recorded retreats in the preceding two centuries, the longest of them fourteen months, and the Court's response was administrative: the Hall of Open Hearing was closed, a notice was posted, the queue was told to come back.

The Duke of Eternal Night was in the room. He has described it exactly twice in three hundred years, both times when very tired, and both accounts agree on three things. The Emperor was carrying nothing. He stopped at the threshold and looked back at the assembled Court for what the Duke estimates was four seconds. And He said something.

The Duke does not know what. He was eleven paces away, the acoustics of the antechamber are poor, and he has spent three hundred years being certain that it was addressed to someone specific and never being able to determine who. He will not speculate about the words. If a player pushes him on this he becomes, uncharacteristically, angry, and the anger is not at the player.

The doors were closed. The watch was set. That was a Tuesday.

#### The Waiting

Two years is a long time to hold a posture.

The first months were ordinary. Business continued, deferred items accumulated, and the phrase _when He returns_ entered every meeting as a scheduling convention, the way one says _after the harvest_. The Court kept His seat. The kitchens continued to prepare His meal on the first and fifteenth, because no one had rescinded the standing order, and the meal was carried to the antechamber and set down and taken away untouched, twice a month, for nine years.

By the second year the phrase _when He returns_ had begun to do something else. It had become a way of not deciding. Every genuinely difficult question in the empire was now deferrable, and everyone discovered, with relief they did not examine, that they preferred deferring.

The queue outside the Hall of Open Hearing did not disperse. This is the part of the record that is hardest to read.
